# Implementation source log

This log continues the spec's source log (Appendix A of `docs/spec/openftgs-spec.md`) for the implementation phase. Every source consulted gets one row: what it was and what was taken from it. Reading this repository and the spec needs no row.

If a forbidden source is met by accident, its row starts with **Incident** and says what was seen, that reading stopped, and that nothing was taken.

| # | Date | Source | What was taken |

| 6 | 2026-10-11 | gsplat 1.5.3 as installed from PyPI (Apache-2.0; `gsplat-1.5.3.dist-info/licenses/LICENSE` read first): `gsplat/cuda/_torch_impl.py`, `_eval_sh_bases_fast` and `_spherical_harmonics`; `gsplat/cuda/_wrapper.py`, the `spherical_harmonics` signature and docstring | The SH basis is ordered by band l and, within a band, m = −l … l; signs keep the Condon–Shortley phase (l = 1 is C₁·(−y, z, −x); the odd-m terms of l = 2 and l = 3 are negative); directions are normalized inside; `spherical_harmonics(degrees_to_use, dirs [..., 3], coeffs [..., K, 3])` returns the plain sum over bases, without the +0.5 offset or a clamp. No code copied: `openftgs.sh` is written from the Cartesian table of the real SH, not from gsplat's recurrence (from #15: M2 · Motion, temporal opacity and spherical harmonics) |

Neither project's documentation nor source was opened (from #15: M2 · Motion, temporal opacity and spherical harmonics) |

the exported stage functions and their signatures; the culls in order (depth, `det <= 0`, opacity below `ALPHA_THRESHOLD` = `1.f/255.f`), the extent `min(3.33, sqrt(2·__logf(opacity/ALPHA_THRESHOLD)))`, `ceilf` radii, the cull when both radii are ≤ `radius_clip`, the off-image test; unpacked `means2d` is allocated with `at::empty` and written only for rows that pass every cull; packed rows are written camera-major in ascending row order by a block scan, with int64 ids; `rasterize_to_pixels` checks the background against `means2d.shape[:-2]`, which rejects a [C, 3] background in packed mode (Q-38); the forward kernel's 0.999 clamp with `__expf`, skip and exclusive stop; the Jacobian-clamp and α_max gradients pass inclusively at the bound; the projection's backward gives no opacity gradient; the SH backward returns the direction gradient; the CUDA code is JIT-built with fast math unless `NO_FAST_MATH=1`, which is why the backends differ in the last bits (Q-39) (from #44: M5 · gsplat renderer) |

Parallel rays through the principal point give a null vector with h₄ = 0 exactly; through other pixels, h₄ of about 10⁻¹⁶ and a finite point about 10¹⁵ away; a zero matrix gives (0, 0, 0, 1). | 627 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv abstract pages https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.17774 (High-quality Surface Reconstruction using Gaussian Surfels, Dai et al., v2), https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.12775 (SuGaR, Guédon and Lepetit, v3), https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.06521 (PGSR, Chen et al., v2), https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.01467 (RaDe-GS, Zhang et al., v2) and https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.10772 (Gaussian Opacity Fields, Yu et al., v2), the five other search seeds of family A, their IDs taken from an arXiv title search and one web search | Titles, authors, versions and abstracts. Gaussian Surfels: the z-scale of a 3D Gaussian is set to 0 and the local z-axis taken as the normal, with a normal–depth consistency loss, monocular normal priors and foreground masks. SuGaR: a regularization term that aligns the Gaussians with the surface, then Poisson meshing and an optional refinement that binds Gaussians to the mesh. PGSR: a depth rendered from the distance of each Gaussian's plane to the camera and a normal map, with single-view geometric, multi-view photometric and multi-view geometric regularization and an exposure model. RaDe-GS: rasterized depth and normal maps for general 3D Gaussians. Gaussian Opacity Fields: an opacity field from ray-traced volume rendering of 3D Gaussians, the normal of the ray–Gaussian intersection plane as the surface normal, and meshing by Marching Tetrahedra (#7) |

The depth distortion term is written with absolute differences in the main text (Eq. 13) and implemented with squared differences of the depths mapped to NDC, summed over ordered pairs with running sums in the kernel (Appendix A, Eq. 17). The normal consistency term weights 1 − nᵢᵀN by the blending weights, N from finite differences of the depth points and nᵢ the splat's normal turned to the camera (Eqs. 14, 15); weights 1000 or 100 and 0.05. Mean and median depth as Appendix B defines them. | 640 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv abstract pages opened for family A's search rounds 1 and 2 (their IDs from a web search's result titles and an arXiv title search), logged on resuming after an interruption: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.08093 (MVGSR), https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.06269 (TriaGS), https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20331 (GVGS), https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20687 (TopoSurfel), https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.17067 (Geometry Field Splatting with Gaussian Surfels), https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.19454 (GausSurf) and https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.15400 (SolidGS) | Titles, dates and abstracts. MVGSR: surface reconstruction among dynamic objects and distractors, with distractor masks from multi-view feature consistency, a pruning measure from multi-view contributions and a multi-view consistency loss. TriaGS (2025-12): a rendered 3D point is penalized for its distance to a consensus point re-triangulated from a bundle of neighbouring views; DTU Chamfer distance 0.50 mm. GVGS (2026-01): visibility modelled per Gaussian primitive, aggregated across views, for a multi-view geometric consistency, and a block-wise affine alignment of monocular depth. TopoSurfel (2026-08): a proxy mesh extracted by differentiable iso-surfacing guides the surfels' normals and their density control. Geometry Field Splatting: a rendering algorithm that splats a geometry field with Gaussian surfels. GausSurf: patch-match multi-view stereo guides texture-rich areas in an iterative scheme, normal priors the texture-less ones. SolidGS: a more solid kernel function for sparse views, with geometric regularization and monocular normals (#7) |
| 641 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv abstract pages opened for search rounds 3 and 4, logged on resuming: https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.11324 (GeoGaussian, ECCV 2024), https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07499 (Trim 3D Gaussian Splatting), https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07337 (From Blobs to Spokes, whose method is named Gaussian Wrapping), https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.05075 (GeoSplat) and https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.17547 (G3Splat) | Titles, dates and abstracts. GeoGaussian: thin Gaussians initialized aligned with surfaces seen in the point cloud, a densification that passes the property on, and explicit geometry constraints. TrimGS: Gaussians are removed by their contribution, scales are kept small, and the method combines with 3DGS and 2DGS. Gaussian Wrapping (2026-04): a learnable oriented normal per Gaussian, an occupancy field, a consistency loss, a densification that closes holes, and a rasterizer changed to output depth as an isosurface. GeoSplat: normals and curvatures estimated from local structure drive initialization, gradient updates and densification, with scales initialized from principal curvatures. G3Splat: priors for feed-forward splatting networks, not for per-scene training (#7) |

No code or project page was opened | A primitive has a time anchor, a lifespan variance and a learnable temporal radius r. Temporal visibility is flat-top (its Eq. 2): 1 within r of the anchor and a Gaussian fall-off in the distance beyond r, scaled by the lifespan variance, outside it. The same two parameters divide the velocity (its Eq. 1), so long-lived primitives stand still. Its regularization (Eqs. 7, 8) has four terms: a flattening term and a single-view normal–depth consistency in the manner of PGSR; an opacity term, the mean of the opacity times the stop-gradient of the temporal visibility, added in the second stage in place of opacity resets; and a lifespan term, the mean over the primitives of the reciprocal of the lifespan (the time at which the fall-off reaches the truncation threshold, plus r), which lengthens lifespans so that one primitive is reused instead of several short ones. The weights are not in the parts read. Densification has two stages: growth for the first third of training, then a fixed count, with low-opacity primitives removed and as many cloned from the top of a score that adds an accumulated error, the opacity and a term favouring fast, short-lived primitives. Initialization reads RAFT flow, SAM 2 masks and COLMAP points; the radius starts at 10⁻⁶. Its data: Neural3DV, 19 to 21 cameras; ENeRF-Outdoor, 18 synchronized cameras; six SelfCap scenes. The regularizer (its Eq. 10) sums over the primitives a term that grows as the effective rank nears 1, max(−log(erank − 1 + ε), 0), and the smallest scale, at weight 0.01, from iteration 7,000; the aim is a rank kept under 2 and away from 1. DTU, mean Chamfer distance and PSNR (Tables 1, 2): 3DGS 1.96 and 32.82 dB; with the changed densification 1.26 and 32.97; with the regularizer too 1.03 and 33.09; SuGaR 1.33 to 1.00, 2DGS 0.80 to 0.77, GOF 0.74 to 0.66 with it. Limits it states: it constrains each primitive alone, knows nothing of the scene's structure and is best paired with a depth-distortion loss; the weight is set by hand and may not suit scenes of thin structures. It argues that monocular depth ratios between the same pixels change between frames and views even after normalization, so a loss on aligned scales makes the 4D geometry inconsistent, while the order holds. No project page or code link on them was followed | Titles and abstracts. 4DSurf: dynamic surface reconstruction with a regularization of the signed-distance flow that the Gaussians' deformation induces, and overlapping segments for large deformations; Hi4D and CMU Panoptic, sparse views. FreeTimeGS++: an analysis from the authors' reproduction of FreeTimeGS; it reports a temporal partitioning driven by the durations and a decoupling of photometric fidelity from motion, and proposes gated marginalization, an initialization guided by UFM and colour correction, for lower run-to-run variance. Dusting the Phantom: two allocation strategies at a fixed budget, new primitives placed from multi-view observations in under-reconstructed dynamic regions, and relocation biased in time. TRiGS: an SE(3) transformation per primitive with Bezier residuals and local anchors, against the fragmentation of piecewise-linear primitives over time. Relaxed Rigidity: Gaussians met by one ray above a blending-weight threshold are grouped and constrained to keep their spatial distribution over time, without flow or tracks. VeGaS: a time-varying velocity as a shear that keeps motion out of the conditional covariance, and a network that refines shapes and orientations. SharpTimeGS: a learnable lifespan that turns the temporal Gaussian into a flat-top profile and modulates motion, with a densification aware of lifespan and velocity. All seven passed the abstract screen. CDGS (a primitive budget and compression) did not. UniFusion aligns monocular depth across cameras and time for sparse-view 4D reconstruction: a technique on prior maps, handed to family C (#10: Surface research · Survey, family D: dynamic-scene surfaces) |

| 768 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2602.02989 (SharpTimeGS: Sharp and Stable Dynamic Gaussian Splatting via Lifespan Modulation, version 2 of 2026-02-05; a paper that cites FreeTimeGS): sections 1, 3, 4 and 5 read. The pages link no code or project page | The method: per primitive, a lifespan variance and a learnable radius r; the temporal opacity is 1 within r of the time anchor and falls off as a Gaussian of the distance beyond r; the position is X + v (t - T) / f with f = 1 + max(1, (variance + r)^2), so a long-lived primitive hardly moves; r starts at 1e-6. Initialization: moving points found with RAFT flow prompt SAM2 for object masks, COLMAP reconstructs the masked objects per frame, nearest neighbours between adjacent frames give the velocity, and the lifespan covers three frames; static points come from the first frame with zero velocity and a lifespan of three times the clip. Densification in two stages: AbsGS's criterion for the first third of training, then a fixed count, with low-opacity primitives removed and as many cloned from the top of a score of accumulated error, opacity and velocity over lifespan. Losses: L1 0.8, SSIM 0.2, perceptual 0.01; a flattening loss on scales and a single-view consistency of normals and depth, both as PGSR's; a loss that is the mean of the reciprocal of the lifespan, to make one primitive serve longer instead of several; an opacity loss of FreeTimeGS's form, with opacity resets stopped. Table 2 (SelfCap, part of it, PSNR): full 27.36; Gaussian profile without r 26.76; 4DGS's coupled representation 25.96; 4DGS's densification 26.82; no velocity-aware initialization 26.83. Table 1: 33.57, 25.82 and 28.14 dB on Neural3DV, ENeRF-Outdoor and SelfCap against FreeTimeGS's published 33.19, 25.36 and 27.50; the authors reproduced FreeTimeGS for the qualitative figures only. Limitation: training takes several hours a scene. | 769 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2603.28064 (4DSurf: High-Fidelity Dynamic Scene Surface Reconstruction, CVPR 2026, version 2 of 2026-10-26; a paper that cites FreeTimeGS): sections 3, 4, 5 and 6 read; the supplementary material was not | The method: 2D Gaussians (2DGS) in a canonical shape per segment of 5 timesteps plus one shared with the next segment; a Gaussian Velocity Field, an MLP of position and time, predicts a velocity, an angular velocity and an expansion velocity per Gaussian, which move its centre, turn its quaternion and change its two scales; the SDF-flow regularization is the absolute difference, per Gaussian centre, between the flow its motion implies (minus the velocity projected on the Gaussian's normal) and the time derivative of an approximate signed distance (the rendered depth at the centre's pixel minus the centre's own depth); segments are trained in order, each started from the previous one's last timestep, with new Gaussians where content appears; later segments may fine-tune the field by LoRA. Other losses: L1 with D-SSIM, 2DGS's normal alignment and depth distortion, and an L1 between rendered alpha and ground-truth masks. Initialization: a visual hull from the first timestep's foreground masks of all views. Built on Dynamic-2DGS's model; each segment trains 30,000 iterations, about 30 minutes on an RTX 3090 Ti; meshes by TSDF fusion. Data: CMU Panoptic (4 scenes, 10 cameras, 24 timesteps) and Hi4D (6 scenes of two people, 8 cameras, 118 timesteps on average), all views used for training, meshes scored by Chamfer distance. Table 3 (Hi4D, overall, cm): velocity field with 2DGS's regularizers 1.49; with the SDF-flow regularization 1.02; with independent segments 0.77; with overlapping segments 0.67; with LoRA 0.70. Tables 1 and 2: FreeTimeGS as a baseline scores 13.1 to 17.9 mm on CMU Panoptic and 1.78 to 3.21 cm on Hi4D, against 10.5 to 12.7 mm and 0.47 to 0.84 cm, and the text says its surfaces jitter and drift over time; how that baseline was run is in the supplementary material, not read. Table 4: the spread of the Chamfer distance over time is 0.18 cm against 1.19 for Dynamic-2DGS. No stated limitations section (#10: Surface research · Survey, family D: dynamic-scene surfaces) |

Losses: L1, SSIM, FreeTimeGS's opacity regularization (0.01), a penalty on the Bezier residual's second difference (1e-4), and a rigidity loss (weight 1) that sums, over each primitive's 3 nearest neighbours by 3D centre, the squared differences of base velocity and base angular velocity, weighted by exp(-50 times the squared difference of DC colours). Relocation every 100 iterations at an opacity threshold of 0.005, at a fixed count of 0.5 M with no densification: sources drawn by a sum of four normalized cues (opacity, speed times temporal scale, accumulated 2D gradient, accumulated temporal gradient), and clones copy every property with scales cut to about 66%. Initialization from RoMa matches; 30,000 iterations on an RTX 4090. Table 4 (SelfCap extended to 1,200 frames, PSNR and primitive count): linear motion 24.83 dB with 2.10 M; rotation and translation as independent parameters about the origin 23.95 with 2.85 M; local se(3) 25.52 with 0.60 M; linear with a Bezier residual 25.10 with 1.85 M; full 26.05 with 0.50 M; the paper says the ablated baselines were allowed to densify and the full model was held at 0.5 M. | 773 | 2026-11-15 | arXiv HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2610.21302 (Dusting the Phantom: Gaussian Allocation for Fading Dynamic Content, version 2 of 2026-10-16; a paper that cites FreeTimeGS and builds on a reproduction of it): the relocation paragraph of section 3.1, sections 3.2, 3.3, 4.1, 4.2, 4.3 and 5, and the parts of appendices A.2 and A.3 on temporal relocation and on the number of cameras, read | Two changes to allocation at a fixed count, the representation unchanged. Temporal Biased Relocation: the primitive to move and its target are chosen as FreeTimeGS does; the copy's time centre is then drawn among the target's observed timestamps in proportion to its mean 2D positional gradient at each, its position is the target's at that time by the linear motion, and its duration is the target's or shorter, so that it stays inside the target's support. Seed Injection, once, from the midpoint of training: a seed map per camera and frame, the L1 error times a mask of pixels that differ between adjacent frames; 48 seed pixels from each of 8 cameras; rays of different cameras paired by their closest approach, consistent pairs kept, and the midpoint taken as a 3D seed, at most 256 a frame; a low-opacity primitive is re-initialized there (isotropic scale from the pixel footprint, velocity from the nearest seed of the next frame, opacity 0.05, duration 0.03), each frame then gets 10 iterations, and relocation stays off for a while. Table 4 (mean of three CMU Panoptic sequences, 500 K primitives, 50,000 iterations at 640 by 360; PSNR, LPIPS, training minutes): baseline 28.22, 0.161, 5.69; with Seed Injection 28.53, 0.160, 5.85; with Temporal Biased Relocation 28.57, 0.080, 5.74; with both 28.98, 0.082, 5.91. Per Gaussian attribute, the two views' gradients conflict when their inner product is negative; they are then rotated in their common plane until orthogonal, magnitudes kept, and for position, rotation and scale the result is attenuated by exp(−k·max(0, −cos θ)). The view-space gradients that drive densification are scaled by the same coefficients. Per Gaussian, a conflict score, the larger of max(0, −cos) over the position's and the opacity's gradients, is averaged over iterations (factor 0.99), and every 100 iterations the opacity is multiplied by exp(−0.3·score), so that persistently conflicting Gaussians fade and are pruned. Masks: a binary prior from SAM refined by a consistency score predicted from DINOv2 features. Table 4 (NeRF On-the-go; PSNR, SSIM, LPIPS, hours): full 23.98, 0.818, 0.123, 0.33 h; without the gradient harmonization 23.50, 0.802, 0.131; without the conflict-aware densification and pruning 23.84, 0.814, 0.128; one view per iteration 23.49, 0.801, 0.139, 0.21 h. Limitation: with unreliable masks, harmonizing two views' gradients is a compromise between inconsistent signals that weakens fine structure.
